People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess the main reason is that I get pulled off track really, really easily. Like, I’ll be trying to finish something—say a dashboard I’m supposed to be working on—and then one Slack ping comes in, or I notice a song lyric in the background, or I suddenly have a random idea for some other project, and it’s like my brain just drops the thread completely.

It’s not even that I’m choosing to stop, exactly. It feels more like I look up and I’m suddenly somewhere else mentally, and then when I come back I have to figure out what I was doing, what tab I was in, what I was trying to solve… and it’s frustrating because the work itself isn’t always the problem. It’s staying with it long enough to actually finish it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, it definitely goes back to when I was a kid. It’s not something that only started now. I remember—or I guess partly I remember and partly my parents have reminded me—that even in elementary school, my teachers would write those comments on report cards like, “rushes through work,” or “needs to slow down and read directions carefully.” I would finish worksheets really fast but then realize I’d skipped part of the instructions or answered the wrong thing because I hadn’t actually paid attention to what it was asking.

And in group work, I got comments about talking over other kids or not waiting my turn. Not like I was trying to be rude, I just… I’d have a thought and say it before thinking about whether someone else was speaking.

Homework was a whole thing too. My parents used to have to sit beside me while I did it, not because I couldn’t understand it, but because I’d just drift off. I’d get up for water, or start sharpening pencils, or suddenly be doing something completely different before the assignment was finished. So, yeah, it’s been there since school years for sure.

Um… no, I don’t think so. Not in that kind of way. I mean, I wasn’t like a kid who was constantly getting into fights or being sent to the principal for serious stuff. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, definitely. It’s not always dramatic, like I’m not necessarily jumping out of my chair every five seconds, but there’s this kind of… internal pressure, I guess, if I have to sit still too long.

Like on a long Zoom call, especially if I’m supposed to just listen and not really do anything, I start feeling like my body is getting sort of uncomfortable from the inside. I’ll be shifting around, changing positions, messing with something on my desk, clicking a pen, picking at my nails, whatever’s nearby. And if I’m trying to quietly work in one place for a long time, I’ll get this urge to get up and pace or go “just do one quick thing,” even if there isn’t really a reason I need to.

As a kid, I think it was similar, but maybe it came out more as squirming or needing to move around. Now I can sometimes make myself stay seated because I know I’m supposed to, but it takes effort, and it’s distracting because part of my brain is just focused on wanting to move.

(Note: In adolescents or adults, may be limited to feeling restless.)\n  manifestation: If I have to sit still through a long Zoom call or quietly work in one place, I start feeling this uncomfortable pressure in my body like I need to get up, pace, or do something with my hands.\n- key: S013\n  name: Quiet_Activity_Difficulty\n  description: Often unable to play or engage in leisure activities quietly.\n  manifestation: Even when I try to relax by reading or doing a calm sketching exercise, I end up talking to myself, playing music, checking something, or turning it into a bigger project instead of just being quiet with it.\n- key: S014\n  name: Constant_Activity\n  description: Is often 'on the go', acting as if 'driven by a motor'.\n  manifestation: On days when I don’t have classes or a formal schedule, I still jump from editing a prototype to cleaning my desk to opening a new dataset, like resting feels weird unless I’m actively doing something.\n- key: S017\n  name: Waiting_Turn_Difficulty\n  description: Often has difficulty waiting his or her turn.\n  manifestation: Waiting for my turn during hackathon check-ins or office-hour queues makes me really antsy, and I catch myself checking the time, shifting around, or wanting to jump in before it’s actually my turn.\n- key: S018\n  name: Interrupting_Intruding\n  description: Often interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n  manifestation: When people are brainstorming, I’ll blurt out my idea or start adding to their Figma board before they’ve finished explaining, and I can tell afterward that I came off as pushy even though I was excited.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Even in elementary school, my teachers wrote comments about me rushing through worksheets, losing track of directions, and talking over classmates during group work. It has also made some friends and collaborators hesitant to work with me, even though they like my ideas.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 5 months\n  manifestation: This has been causing real problems for about a year and five months, since I moved from structured school projects into building my freelance UX and data-visualization portfolio.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 19 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S008\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you noticed this kind of difficulty staying focused as far back as childhood or your school years, or is this something that started more recently in adulthood?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it definitely goes back to when I was a kid. It’s not something that only started now. I remember—or I guess partly I remember and partly my parents have reminded me—that even in elementary school, my teachers would write those comments on report cards like, “rushes through work,” or “needs to slow down and read directions carefully.” I would finish worksheets really fast but then realize I’d skipped part of the instructions or answered the wrong thing because I hadn’t actually paid attention to what it was asking.\n\nAnd in group work, I got comments about talking over other kids or not waiting my turn. Not like I was trying to be rude, I just… I’d have a thought and say it before thinking about whether someone else was speaking.\n\nHomework was a whole thing too. My parents used to have to sit beside me while I did it, not because I couldn’t understand it, but because I’d just drift off. I’d get up for water, or start sharpening pencils, or suddenly be doing something completely different before the assignment was finished. So, yeah, it’s been there since school years for sure.\n\nDoctor: Growing up, did you have significant behavior problems—like frequent fighting, defying authority, breaking rules, or getting into serious trouble—separate from the distractibility you've described?\n\nPatient: Um… no, I don’t think so. Not in that kind of way. I mean, I wasn’t like a kid who was constantly getting into fights or being sent to the principal for serious stuff. I could be annoying in class, probably, or talk when I wasn’t supposed to, but I don’t remember it being like I was deliberately trying to defy teachers or break rules.\n\nI was more the kind of kid who would get corrected for blurting something out or not following directions, and then feel embarrassed afterward. I don’t think there was anything like stealing, running away, fighting, or getting suspended or anything like that. At least not that I know of. If my parents had some different view I guess they might remember more, but from what I remember, no, it wasn’t really “behavior problems” in that serious sense.\n\nDoctor: Besides blurting things out, have you also struggled with physical restlessness—like feeling like you need to move, fidgeting, or having a hard time staying seated for long periods, both as a child and now?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It’s not always dramatic, like I’m not necessarily jumping out of my chair every five seconds, but there’s this kind of… internal pressure, I guess, if I have to sit still too long.\n\nLike on a long Zoom call, especially if I’m supposed to just listen and not really do anything, I start feeling like my body is getting sort of uncomfortable from the inside. I’ll be shifting around, changing positions, messing with something on my desk, clicking a pen, picking at my nails, whatever’s nearby. And if I’m trying to quietly work in one place for a long time, I’ll get this urge to get up and pace or go “just do one quick thing,” even if there isn’t really a reason I need to.\n\nAs a kid, I think it was similar, but maybe it came out more as squirming or needing to move around. Now I can sometimes make myself stay seated because I know I’m supposed to, but it takes effort, and it’s distracting because part of my brain is just focused on wanting to move.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say these focus and restlessness issues are currently causing real problems in more than one area of your life—like both at work and in your personal relationships or home life?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I would say it’s causing real problems, not just like “I’m a little scattered” problems. Even though I’m not in a regular job right now, I’ve been trying to do freelance-type creative work and build my portfolio, and that’s where it really shows up.

I’ve lost a couple of small freelance contracts because people couldn’t really rely on me to follow through cleanly. Like, I’d have good ideas and I’d be excited at the beginning, but then the actual finishing, responding, keeping track of little details, turning things in the way they asked… that’s where it would fall apart. And I missed out on a portfolio competition too, which still bothers me, because it wasn’t that I didn’t care. I did care. I just didn’t manage the process well enough.

And socially, or with friends and collaborators, it affects things too. Some people like my ideas and like working with me in theory, but they’re hesitant to actually collaborate because they’re not sure I’ll be consistent or follow through without things getting messy. That feels really embarrassing, honestly, because it makes me seem careless when I’m not trying to be. So yeah, it’s definitely showing up in more than one area.

Um… I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I haven’t noticed some big recent change where I can point to it and say, “Oh, that’s why this is happening.” Like, I don’t feel like there was a clear before-and-after shift recently.

It’s pretty much most days, honestly. It’s not only when I’m especially stressed or in a bad mood, although stress definitely makes it more obvious.

The main thing—and honestly one of the biggest reasons I came in—is that I get pulled off track so easily. Like I can be in the middle of trying to finish a dashboard or some kind of project, and one tiny thing breaks the thread. A Slack ping, or even just seeing a notification pop up, or a song lyric in the background catching my attention, or I’ll suddenly have a random idea for another project and my brain just… goes there. And then I look back and realize I’ve completely lost what I was doing.

It feels like I’m constantly trying to hold the task in my head, and any little thing can knock it out of my hands, basically. So it’s not just one setting. It happens when I’m working on stuff, but also at home or when I’m trying to read or follow something. It comes and goes in intensity, but the distractibility itself feels pretty consistent.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… at least not in the way you’re describing it. I’ve definitely had times where I get really excited about an idea and I’ll feel kind of mentally “on,” like I want to start a bunch of things at once, but I wouldn’t say it’s this distinct several-day period where I’m unusually euphoric or a totally different version of myself.

And the sleep part especially—I don’t think I’ve had times where I truly needed much less sleep and felt great or super energized from that. If I sleep less, I usually just feel worse or more scattered. I can get irritable when I’m overwhelmed, but it feels tied to frustration or stress, not like a separate elevated mood state.

So… no, nothing really jumps out to me as a clear episode like that. If anything, it’s more like bursts of excitement or restlessness that come and go, not days of feeling unusually high-energy and not needing sleep.

My parents used to have to sit beside me while I did it, not because I couldn’t understand it, but because I’d just drift off. I’d get up for water, or start sharpening pencils, or suddenly be doing something completely different before the assignment was finished. So, yeah, it’s been there since school years for sure.\n\nDoctor: Growing up, did you have significant behavior problems—like frequent fighting, defying authority, breaking rules, or getting into serious trouble—separate from the distractibility you've described?\n\nPatient: Um… no, I don’t think so. Not in that kind of way. I mean, I wasn’t like a kid who was constantly getting into fights or being sent to the principal for serious stuff. I could be annoying in class, probably, or talk when I wasn’t supposed to, but I don’t remember it being like I was deliberately trying to defy teachers or break rules.\n\nI was more the kind of kid who would get corrected for blurting something out or not following directions, and then feel embarrassed afterward. I don’t think there was anything like stealing, running away, fighting, or getting suspended or anything like that. At least not that I know of. If my parents had some different view I guess they might remember more, but from what I remember, no, it wasn’t really “behavior problems” in that serious sense.\n\nDoctor: Besides blurting things out, have you also struggled with physical restlessness—like feeling like you need to move, fidgeting, or having a hard time staying seated for long periods, both as a child and now?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It’s not always dramatic, like I’m not necessarily jumping out of my chair every five seconds, but there’s this kind of… internal pressure, I guess, if I have to sit still too long.\n\nLike on a long Zoom call, especially if I’m supposed to just listen and not really do anything, I start feeling like my body is getting sort of uncomfortable from the inside. I’ll be shifting around, changing positions, messing with something on my desk, clicking a pen, picking at my nails, whatever’s nearby. And if I’m trying to quietly work in one place for a long time, I’ll get this urge to get up and pace or go “just do one quick thing,” even if there isn’t really a reason I need to.\n\nAs a kid, I think it was similar, but maybe it came out more as squirming or needing to move around. Now I can sometimes make myself stay seated because I know I’m supposed to, but it takes effort, and it’s distracting because part of my brain is just focused on wanting to move.\n\nDoctor: Would you say these focus and restlessness issues are currently causing real problems in more than one area of your life—like both at work and in your personal relationships or home life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I would say it’s causing real problems, not just like “I’m a little scattered” problems. Even though I’m not in a regular job right now, I’ve been trying to do freelance-type creative work and build my portfolio, and that’s where it really shows up.\n\nI’ve lost a couple of small freelance contracts because people couldn’t really rely on me to follow through cleanly. Like, I’d have good ideas and I’d be excited at the beginning, but then the actual finishing, responding, keeping track of little details, turning things in the way they asked… that’s where it would fall apart. And I missed out on a portfolio competition too, which still bothers me, because it wasn’t that I didn’t care. I did care. I just didn’t manage the process well enough.\n\nAnd socially, or with friends and collaborators, it affects things too. Some people like my ideas and like working with me in theory, but they’re hesitant to actually collaborate because they’re not sure I’ll be consistent or follow through without things getting messy. That feels really embarrassing, honestly, because it makes me seem careless when I’m not trying to be. So yeah, it’s definitely showing up in more than one area.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any recent changes in your mood, anxiety levels, or sleep, or do you use alcohol, caffeine, or other substances regularly, that might be contributing to how you're feeling?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I haven’t noticed some big recent change where I can point to it and say, “Oh, that’s why this is happening.” Like, I don’t feel like there was a clear before-and-after shift recently.\n\nMy mood and anxiety can fluctuate in normal ways, I guess, especially when I’m stressed about things I’m behind on, but I don’t know that it feels like a separate thing causing all of this. And with sleep or caffeine or alcohol or anything like that… I don’t think there’s anything obvious I’d know to blame. I mean, I’m sure if I sleep badly I’m worse the next day, but I haven’t noticed a specific pattern that explains the bigger problem.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: not that I’ve clearly noticed. If there’s something specific you mean by contributing, I can try to think through it more, but nothing jumps out to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice these attention and restlessness difficulties happening pretty much every day and in most settings—like at home, when reading for pleasure, or during conversations with family—or does it seem to come and go depending on your mood or stress level?\n\nPatient: It’s pretty much most days, honestly. It’s not only when I’m especially stressed or in a bad mood, although stress definitely makes it more obvious.\n\nThe main thing—and honestly one of the biggest reasons I came in—is that I get pulled off track so easily. Like I can be in the middle of trying to finish a dashboard or some kind of project, and one tiny thing breaks the thread. A Slack ping, or even just seeing a notification pop up, or a song lyric in the background catching my attention, or I’ll suddenly have a random idea for another project and my brain just… goes there. And then I look back and realize I’ve completely lost what I was doing.\n\nIt feels like I’m constantly trying to hold the task in my head, and any little thing can knock it out of my hands, basically. So it’s not just one setting. It happens when I’m working on stuff, but also at home or when I’m trying to read or follow something. It comes and goes in intensity, but the distractibility itself feels pretty consistent.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had distinct periods—lasting several days or more—where you felt unusually elevated, euphoric, or irritable, needed much less sleep than usual, and had a surge of energy or racing thoughts, that were different from your usual self?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in the way you’re describing it. I’ve definitely had times where I get really excited about an idea and I’ll feel kind of mentally “on,” like I want to start a bunch of things at once, but I wouldn’t say it’s this distinct several-day period where I’m unusually euphoric or a totally different version of myself.\n\nAnd the sleep part especially—I don’t think I’ve had times where I truly needed much less sleep and felt great or super energized from that. If I sleep less, I usually just feel worse or more scattered. I can get irritable when I’m overwhelmed, but it feels tied to frustration or stress, not like a separate elevated mood state.\n\nSo… no, nothing really jumps out to me as a clear episode like that. If anything, it’s more like bursts of excitement or restlessness that come and go, not days of feeling unusually high-energy and not needing sleep.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation. Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. I remember—or I guess partly I remember and partly my parents have reminded me—that even in elementary school, my teachers would write those comments on report cards like, “rushes through work,” or “needs to slow down and read directions carefully.” I would finish worksheets really fast but then realize I’d skipped part of the instructions or answered the wrong thing because I hadn’t actually paid attention to what it was asking.\n\nAnd in group work, I got comments about talking over other kids or not waiting my turn. Not like I was trying to be rude, I just… I’d have a thought and say it before thinking about whether someone else was speaking.\n\nHomework was a whole thing too. My parents used to have to sit beside me while I did it, not because I couldn’t understand it, but because I’d just drift off. I’d get up for water, or start sharpening pencils, or suddenly be doing something completely different before the assignment was finished. So, yeah, it’s been there since school years for sure.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
]

Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event. Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame). Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nUm… no, I don’t think so. Not in that kind of way. I mean, I wasn’t like a kid who was constantly getting into fights or being sent to the principal for serious stuff. I could be annoying in class, probably, or talk when I wasn’t supposed to, but I don’t remember it being like I was deliberately trying to defy teachers or break rules.\n\nI was more the kind of kid who would get corrected for blurting something out or not following directions, and then feel embarrassed afterward. I don’t think there was anything like stealing, running away, fighting, or getting suspended or anything like that. At least not that I know of.   }

Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nYeah, definitely. It’s not always dramatic, like I’m not necessarily jumping out of my chair every five seconds, but there’s this kind of… internal pressure, I guess, if I have to sit still too long.\n\nLike on a long Zoom call, especially if I’m supposed to just listen and not really do anything, I start feeling like my body is getting sort of uncomfortable from the inside. I’ll be shifting around, changing positions, messing with something on my desk, clicking a pen, picking at my nails, whatever’s nearby. And if I’m trying to quietly work in one place for a long time, I’ll get this urge to get up and pace or go “just do one quick thing,” even if there isn’t really a reason I need to.\n\nAs a kid, I think it was similar, but maybe it came out more as squirming or needing to move around. Now I can sometimes make myself stay seated because I know I’m supposed to, but it takes effort, and it’s distracting because part of my brain is just focused on wanting to move.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt. Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy. Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected. Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.
